FILE: README.md
================================================
# narxnoma — price-fetch engine for Uzbekistan's electronics retailers

Eleven verified retailer clients, one `Listing` contract, and a Claude Code
plugin whose skills tell an agent exactly how each store is fetched: the
endpoint, the auth, which field is the price and in what unit, how to build a
URL that opens, and the traps that silently produce wrong numbers.

Aggregator only — it reads public catalogues; it never sells.

> The price we show belongs to the exact product you are looking at, and we
> can show you which listing it came from.

## Sources

| slug | site | kind | auth | sweep | search |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| uzum | uzum.uz | GraphQL, multi-seller, per-SKU | anonymous self-minted token | yes | yes |
| alifshop | alifshop.uz | REST POST, multi-seller, price in tiyin | none | yes | yes |
| beemarket | market.beeline.uz | REST GET, category endpoint | none | yes | yes |
| idea | idea.uz | REST GET, brand queries, no pagination | none | yes | yes |
| mediapark | mediapark.uz | REST GET autocomplete, ~10 hits | none | **no** | yes |
| olcha | olcha.uz | REST GET, full catalogue | none | yes | client-side |
| texnomart | texnomart.uz | REST GET, broad query | none | yes | yes |
| asaxiy | asaxiy.uz | server-rendered HTML | none | yes | no |
| radius | radius.uz | Saleor GraphQL, one row per variant | none | yes | yes |
| glotr | glotr.uz | server-rendered HTML classifieds | none — **robots.txt disallows; permission required** | yes | no |
| openshop | openshop.uz | REST POST, full catalogue in ~120 requests | none | yes | yes (fuzzy) |

Categories: smartphones, televisions, laptops, acs, fridges, washers, ovens.

## Quick start — prices in one command, no database

```bash
git clone https://github.com/ShaKQl/narxnoma-claude-plugin
cd narxnoma-claude-plugin
pip install -e narxnoma-crawler            # Python >= 3.11; requests + psycopg
cd narxnoma-crawler

python -m narxnoma.cli fetch --source olcha --limit 20
python -m narxnoma.cli fetch --source uzum --category laptops > uzum_laptops.jsonl
python -m narxnoma.cli fetch --source mediapark --query "iphone 16"      # search-only source
python -m narxnoma.cli fetch --source texnomart --raw                     # keep the retailer payload
```

Output is JSON lines, one listing per line:

```json
{"store_slug": "texnomart", "source_key": "359853", "name_raw": "Vivo V70 FE 8/256GB Blue Smartfoni",
 "price": 6111000, "url": "https://texnomart.uz/product/detail/359853/", "availability": "UNKNOWN",
 "image_url": "https://mini-io-api.texnomart.uz/...", "category": "smartphones"}
```

`price` is an integer in UZS. Every value is what the retailer stated; nothing
is inferred at this layer.

## Use it as a Claude Code plugin

```
/plugin marketplace add ShaKQl/narxnoma-claude-plugin
/plugin install narxnoma@narxnoma
```

Then ask Claude for prices from any of the stores, or to add a new one. Skills:

| skill | when it loads |
|---|---|
| `narxnoma-fetch` | any request for Uzbek retailer prices; running or debugging the engine |
| `fetch-uzum` … `fetch-openshop` | one per store: endpoint, headers, price field and unit, URL rule, traps, fixture and tests |
| `adding-a-narxnoma-source` | adding a retailer; a site that looks unscrapable |
| `narxnoma-market-research` | researching a product list against the markets; delivering an analysable Excel file |
| `narxnoma-excel-workbooks` | generating Excel with PivotTables, slicers, formulas, conditional formatting; verifying it in Excel |

The skills are operational cards. `docs/parsers/` is the rebuild-grade source
of truth they point to; every client here was re-implemented from those notes
alone and asserted live.

## Market research — a product list in, an analysable workbook out

Give it a list of products (`.xlsx` / `.csv` / `.txt`); it sweeps the markets, matches every
product with the shared identity matcher, keeps **every** accepted offer, and writes a workbook
you can work in rather than a static table:

```bash
pip install -e "narxnoma-crawler[excel]"      # adds openpyxl
cd narxnoma-crawler
python -m narxnoma.research run --targets products.xlsx --out research.xlsx
python -m narxnoma.research check research.xlsx      # real Excel recalculates it, reports errors
```

- **A database**: Excel Tables `tblOffers` / `tblProducts` / `tblMarkets`; your own columns
  become pivot dimensions.
- **Formulas that follow edits**: change *Our price*, a threshold or the baseline, or add an
  offer row, and the grid, deltas, bands and colours all update.
- **A heat map that is conditional formatting**, per-row baseline, thresholds on a Settings sheet.
- **Real PivotTables**: Excel COM adds three pivots, slicers, a pivot chart and (if Power Pivot
  exists) a Data Model with DAX; with no Excel the pivots are written straight into the package
  and build when the file opens.

Details: `docs/market-research.md`; the Excel engineering: `docs/excel-workbooks.md`.

## Full pipeline (with PostgreSQL)

`harvest → cluster → aggregate → report`, turning per-store listings into
canonical products with a robust price per variant and an audit trail for
every attachment.

```bash
export NARXNOMA_DSN=postgresql://narxnoma:narxnoma@localhost:5432/narxnoma
python -m narxnoma.cli cycle          # all stages, isolated; non-zero if any source is suspicious
python -m narxnoma.cli report         # coverage and freshness per source
```

Local Postgres setup: `docs/deployment/local-postgres.md`.

## Layout

| Path | What it is |
|---|---|
| `skills/` | Claude Code skills — one per store, one umbrella, one for adding a store |
| `.claude-plugin/` | plugin and marketplace manifests |
| `docs/parsers/` | canonical notes per retailer + the shared matching engine |
| `narxnoma-crawler/` | the engine: `sources/`, `matching/`, `research/`, `pipeline/`, `cli.py`, fixture-backed tests |
| `narxnoma-seed/` | pre-production matcher + 14,860-row golden corpus (reference data) |
| `narxnoma-web/` | optional Next.js frontend over the Postgres catalogue |
| `docs/superpowers/` | design specs and implementation plans |
| `docs/CHANGELOG.md` | what changed, newest first, with the current state and open items |
| `docs/folder-map.md` | where everything lives and the naming conventions |
| `tools/` | maintenance scripts (digest regeneration) |
| `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/skills/narxnoma-maintain/` | start here when *working on* the repo with an agent |

## Tests

```bash
cd narxnoma-crawler
python -m pytest -q                                     # offline, fixture-backed
python -m pytest -q -m live tests/sources/test_contract_live.py   # hits the retailers
python -m pytest -q -m excel tests/research                       # needs desktop Excel (Windows)
```

## Working rules

1. `docs/parsers/` is canonical. If implementation and doc disagree, verify
   live, then fix both in the same commit.
2. Never sacrifice precision for recall. Colour is a hard identity field;
   unknown colour never equals any colour.
3. Every rejection must be explainable. Nothing is inferred that cannot later
   be audited.
4. A transport check cannot validate a matcher, and aggregate metrics cannot
   see a substitution. Diff against an independent copy of the same fact.
5. Be polite: sequential requests, 0.3–0.6 s between them, a real
   `User-Agent`, the `Origin`/`Referer` the site's own app sends. Respect
   `robots.txt`; where it forbids crawling, get permission or stop.

## For AI agents and LLM tools

- `llms.txt` — index of what to read first, per the llmstxt convention.
- `llms-engine.txt` — gitingest digest of docs/parsers, skills and the engine source (~140k tokens).
- `llms-full.txt` — gitingest digest of the whole repo minus recorded fixtures and the golden corpus (~300k tokens).
- Regenerate after changes: `pip install gitingest && python -m gitingest . -o llms-full.txt` (`.gitingestignore` is honoured), or paste the repo URL into https://gitingest.com.

# narxnoma

> Price-fetch engine for Uzbekistan's electronics retailers (uzum, alifshop, beemarket, idea, mediapark, olcha, texnomart, asaxiy, radius, glotr, openshop), packaged as a Claude Code plugin. Every client was rebuilt from the notes in docs/parsers/ and asserted live; those notes are canonical.

Start with the README, then the umbrella skill, then the per-store note for the retailer in question. `python -m narxnoma.cli fetch --source SLUG` prints one source's listings as JSON lines with no database.

## Read first

- [README](README.md): quick start, source table, plugin install, layout
- [narxnoma-fetch skill](skills/narxnoma-fetch/SKILL.md): engine layout, Listing contract, rules every source obeys
- [Parser notes index](docs/parsers/README.md): the one thing to understand first, the two shapes of retailer, rules for every source
- [Matching engine](docs/parsers/_matching-engine.md): name to (brand, model, memory, colour) identity and scoring
- [Market research](docs/market-research.md): a product list in, an analysable workbook out (Tables, formulas, heat map, PivotTables)
- [Excel workbooks](docs/excel-workbooks.md): building analysable .xlsx from Python; COM pivots, raw-OOXML pivots, conditional formatting, verifying in Excel
- [Changelog](docs/CHANGELOG.md): latest state, open items, history by era

## Per-store notes (canonical)

- [uzum](docs/parsers/uzum.md): GraphQL, anonymous self-minted token, SKU price not card price
- [alifshop](docs/parsers/alifshop.md): REST POST, price in tiyin, /moderated-offer/ URL
- [beemarket](docs/parsers/beemarket.md): REST GET, category endpoint for sweeps
- [idea](docs/parsers/idea.md): REST GET, brand queries, 404 on empty search
- [mediapark](docs/parsers/mediapark.md): autocomplete only, slug lies
- [olcha](docs/parsers/olcha.md): full-catalogue pull, per_page capped at 20
- [texnomart](docs/parsers/texnomart.md): broad query sweep, not-a-phone denylist
- [asaxiy](docs/parsers/asaxiy.md): server-rendered HTML, /ru/ path, page-1 redirect trap
- [radius](docs/parsers/radius.md): Saleor GraphQL, variant is the offer, default-variant URL
- [glotr](docs/parsers/glotr.md): server-rendered classifieds; robots.txt forbids, permission required
- [openshop](docs/parsers/openshop.md): REST POST full-catalogue sweep, 48/page and ~2,400 deep-paging caps, ?lang=ru URLs

## Per-store skills (operational cards)

- [skills/](skills/): fetch-<slug>/SKILL.md for each store, plus adding-a-narxnoma-source, narxnoma-market-research, narxnoma-excel-workbooks

## Code

- [sources/](narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/sources/): one client per store; base.py holds Listing, coerce_price, paginate
- [matching/](narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/matching/): normalisation, features, brands, colours, scoring
- [pipeline/](narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/pipeline/): harvest, cluster, aggregate, quality, report
- [research/](narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/research/): targets, collect, match, workbook, excel pivots (`python -m narxnoma.research`)
- [cli.py](narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/cli.py): fetch | harvest | cluster | aggregate | quality | report | cycle | research
- [Design spec](docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-27-narxnoma-design.md)

## Whole-repo digests (gitingest)

- [llms-engine.txt](llms-engine.txt): docs/parsers + skills + engine source, no tests or web (~140k tokens)
- [llms-full.txt](llms-full.txt): everything except recorded fixtures and the golden corpus (~300k tokens)
- Regenerate both: `pip install gitingest && python tools/regen_digests.py` (respects .gitingestignore); or open https://gitingest.com/ShaKQl/narxnoma-claude-plugin

- [narxnoma-web/](narxnoma-web/): Next.js frontend over the Postgres catalogue
- [narxnoma-seed/](narxnoma-seed/): pre-production matcher and golden corpus

FILE: docs/excel-workbooks.md

# Building analysable Excel workbooks

What it takes to generate an `.xlsx` that is a **tool**, not a screenshot of numbers: a
database you can pivot, formulas that follow edits, conditional formatting that stays honest,
and real PivotTables. Everything here was learned building
`narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/research/` and was verified against real desktop Excel 16; the
failures are recorded because each one cost an hour. The workflow that uses it is
[market-research.md](market-research.md).

The techniques are generalised from tooling that builds PivotTables over an OLAP cube through
Excel automation (hidden Excel via COM, `GETPIVOTDATA`-linked report cells, recalculating a
file by letting Excel do it). No data from that work is in this repo.

## 1. The one ordering rule

```
openpyxl builds   →   pivots / slicers / Data Model are added   →   nothing re-saves through openpyxl
```

openpyxl can neither create a PivotTable nor keep a slicer: it warns *"Slicer List extension
is not supported and will be removed"* on load and silently drops slicers and the Data Model
on save. So the sheets, Tables, formulas, validation, conditional formatting and charts are
written first; the pivot step runs last and owns the file from then on. A pipeline that
loads the finished workbook with openpyxl "just to tweak a cell" destroys it.

## 2. Excel Tables are the database

- One Table per entity (`tblOffers`, `tblProducts`, `tblMarkets`), one fact per row. A flat
  long table (one row per product × market offer) is what a PivotTable wants; a wide grid is
  a *view* of it, built with formulas.
- **A Table column's name must equal its header cell's text, exactly.** `openpyxl` writes
  `Column1…ColumnN` if you call `table._initialise_columns()` (needed to attach
  `calculatedColumnFormula`) and Excel then refuses to open the file with an unhelpful
  "cannot get Open property" error. Restore the names from the header cells right after
  (`_calc_columns` in `research/workbook.py`).
- **Calculated columns** need `TableFormula(attr_text=...)` on the column so a row a person
  adds to the Table fills itself in. The formula text is stored *without* the leading `=`
  and uses `Table[[#This Row],[Col]]`.
- Keep referenced header names free of special characters (`( ) % # [ ] ' "`): they need
  escaping inside structured references. Spaces are fine. `Delta vs baseline`, not `Δ%`.
- Name the knobs: thresholds and switches live in a `Settings` sheet and are **defined
  names** (`cfg_sat`), so formulas, conditional-format rules and pivots read the same cell.
  Add data validation (decimal 0–1, a list) so a typo cannot produce nonsense.
- Inputs are visibly inputs (pale-yellow fill). Everything else is a formula.

## 3. Formulas: what the file format needs that the UI hides

- **`_xlfn.` prefix** for functions newer than Excel 2007 written through openpyxl:
  `_xlfn.MINIFS`, `_xlfn.MAXIFS`, `_xlfn.IFS`, `_xlfn.XLOOKUP`… Without it the cell shows
  `#NAME?` until someone re-enters it. `COUNTIFS`, `SUMIFS`, `AVERAGEIFS`, `IFERROR`,
  `AGGREGATE` are fine bare. A test asserts no bare `MINIFS` is ever written.
- openpyxl writes **no cached values**. Set `fullCalcOnLoad` so Excel calculates on open, and
  know that a previewer, pandas or LibreOffice-less reader sees blanks. To bake values in,
  let Excel save the file (`verify.ps1 -Save`).
- A SUMPRODUCT of `(col=x)/COUNTIFS(col,col, …)` gives a distinct count in every Excel
  version; `INDEX(range,0,MATCH(name,header,0))` returns a whole column by header name,
  which is how one formula fills a table row per market.
- Use `""` (not 0) for "no data"; `COUNT`, `MEDIAN`, `AVERAGE` ignore text. Never write 0
  for "not found": zero is a price.

## 4. Conditional formatting that cannot go stale

A painted fill is a second copy of a fact, and it goes stale the moment the number is edited.
Use rules.

- **Per-row baseline** (what a colour scale cannot do — its end points come from the whole
  range): a formula rule with a relative row and absolute column, applied to the block:
  `AND(ISNUMBER(D2),N($N2)>0,D2<=$N2*(1-cfg_sat))`. Thresholds from defined names. Six graded
  rules with `stopIfTrue`, plus one non-stopping rule (bold border on `D2=MIN($D2:$M2)`) so
  both apply to the same cell.
- Rule fills need **`bgColor`** (set both `fgColor` and `bgColor`): Excel reads a conditional
  `dxf` solid fill from `bgColor`.
- **Graded colour scale with editable end points**: `ColorScaleRule(start_type="formula",
  start_value="-cfg_sat", mid_type="num", mid_value=0, end_type="formula",
  end_value="cfg_sat")` — a defined name works as a cfvo formula, so the scale follows
  Settings.
- **On a PivotTable** (COM): `DataBodyRange.FormatConditions.AddColorScale(3)` then
  `ScopeType = 2` (`xlDataFieldScope`) so the scale follows the pivot as it is rearranged
  rather than being tied to the cells it happened to cover.
- Direction must be stated in the file (Notes + a legend on Settings): here red = the market is
  *cheaper* than the baseline.

## 5. PivotTables through COM

Driven from PowerShell (`research/excel/pivots.ps1`); Python only launches it.

- **Every COM call retries on `0x800AC472` ("busy")** (plus `RPC_E_*`, `0x8001010A`): Excel
  answers that intermittently while it is calculating or refreshing, and one unwrapped call
  fails the whole build. Any other error is raised with the step's name.
- Calculate first (`CalculateFull`, wait for `CalculationState = 0`): a pivot built over
  unevaluated formulas is empty.
- Source = the Table by name: `PivotCaches().Create(1, "tblOffers")` (`xlDatabase`). Create
  **one cache** and several pivots from it: slicers connect to pivots on the same cache, so
  one slicer drives them all (`SlicerCaches.Add2(pt, "Field")`, then
  `.PivotTables.AddPivotTable(other)`).
- Number formats on the **data field** (`DataField.NumberFormat`), not painted on cells.
- **Captions follow the Excel UI language** — a Russian Excel prints "Названия строк" and
  "Общий итог". Set `CompactLayoutRowHeader`, `CompactLayoutColumnHeader` and
  `GrandTotalName` so the workbook reads the same everywhere. Field names are the Table's own
  headers and are unaffected.
- Slicers: place them right of *both* the pivot and the chart below it; a pivot narrows after
  its captions are shortened, so compute the position after formatting.
- Pivot chart: legend at the bottom, `ShowAllFieldButtons = $false` (the slicers filter).
- **Optional steps never abort the build**: wrap style, colour scale, chart in a `Try-Step`
  that prints `WARN` and goes on; wrap required steps so a failure names the step.
- Always `Quit()` and release the COM object in `finally`, or a hidden `EXCEL.EXE` stays alive
  holding the file.

### Data Model (Power Pivot), probed

```
$wb.Model                                   # null / absent without Power Pivot → fall back to flat pivots
$wb.Connections.Add2("WorksheetConnection_tblX", "", "WORKSHEET;$file", "tblX", 7, $true, $false)
                                            # 7 = xlCmdExcel, True = add to the Data Model
$m.ModelRelationships.Add($fkColumn, $pkColumn)
$m.ModelMeasures.Add("Name", $table, "DAX…", $m.ModelFormatDecimalNumber(1,0), "description")
$pc = $wb.PivotCaches().Create(2, $wb.Connections.Item("ThisWorkbookDataModel"))
$pt.CubeFields.Item("[tblMarkets].[Market]").Orientation = 1
$pt.CubeFields.Item("[Measures].[Avg delta]").Orientation = 4
```

Do it in a `try`, and keep the flat pivots regardless: the flat tier is the guaranteed one.

## 6. PivotTables with no Excel (raw OOXML)

`research/excel/ooxml_pivot.py` writes the parts into the package and marks the cache
`refreshOnLoad="1"`, so Excel builds the pivots on first open (the same approach libraries
like EPPlus use). It needs: `pivotCacheDefinition` (source `<worksheetSource name="tblOffers"/>`,
one `cacheField` per Table column with empty `sharedItems`), an empty `pivotCacheRecords`
part, one `pivotTableDefinition` per pivot (axis fields carry just `<item t="default"/>`;
`rowFields`/`colFields`/`dataFields` declared; no `rowItems`/`colItems`), the relationships,
`[Content_Types].xml` overrides, and `<pivotCaches>` in `workbook.xml` after `<calcPr>`.

The first attempt (building the objects with `openpyxl.pivot`) produced a file Excel would not
open. The hand-written version failed the same way once, for a reason worth keeping: openpyxl's
`workbook.xml` declares `xmlns:r` only on each `<sheet>` element, so a bare
`<pivotCache r:id="…">` used an **undeclared prefix** and the package was invalid. Declare the
namespace on the element. Excel gives no diagnostic through COM — bisect by removing parts.

Limits: no slicers, no pivot chart, no Data Model, built-in number formats only
(`numFmtId` 3 = `#,##0`, 10 = `0.00%`).

## 7. Prove it in Excel, not in Python

openpyxl cannot evaluate a formula, so only Excel can say the file is valid.
`research/excel/verify.ps1`:

- opens, `CalculateFull`, then `UsedRange.SpecialCells(xlCellTypeFormulas, xlErrors)` per
  sheet — counts and names the first error cells;
- reports `ListObjects`, `PivotTables`, `SlicerCaches`, `FormatConditions` counts;
- `-Probe "Prices!D2,…"` returns each cell's **displayed** text and
  `DisplayFormat.Interior.Color` — the colour a conditional-format rule actually produces;
- `-Save` bakes the values in.

Then **recompute independently** in Python from the Offers rows and compare (grid, medians,
baselines, deltas, bands, per-market stats). Then **look**: `Worksheet.ExportAsFixedFormat`
to PDF, render with PyMuPDF, read the PNG — that is how an overlapping chart, an invisible
axis and a localised caption were found. Finally test *behaviour*: change an input through
COM (`Range.Value2`), recalc, probe the colours again.

## 8. Traps, one line each

| Symptom | Cause |
|---|---|
| Excel: "cannot get Open property of Workbooks" | Table column names ≠ header text; or an undeclared XML namespace prefix; bisect by deleting parts |
| `#NAME?` in a formula that looks right | newer function without `_xlfn.` |
| Pivot sheet empty | built before `CalculateFull`; or `refreshOnLoad` pivot over uncalculated formulas |
| Slicers vanished | the file was re-saved through openpyxl |
| Bar chart has no axis labels | openpyxl ≥ 3.1 hides axes: `axis.delete = False` |
| Colour rule shows no fill | solid fill without `bgColor` in a conditional `dxf` |
| "Row Labels" / "Общий итог" in the pivot | caption follows Excel's UI language: set the captions |
| COM call fails intermittently | `0x800AC472`: retry wrapper |
| Hidden `EXCEL.EXE` left running | `Quit()` not in `finally` |
| Colours "wrong" after sorting | baked fills; use rules |



================================================
FILE: docs/market-research.md
================================================
# Market research: a product list in, an analysable workbook out

Canonical note for `narxnoma-crawler/narxnoma/research/`. Read it before changing that
package. The Excel engineering behind the workbook is in
[excel-workbooks.md](excel-workbooks.md).

## What it answers

*"Here is a list of products. What does every market charge for them, and where do we
stand?"* — and it hands back a workbook you can **work in**, not a picture of the numbers:
a small database (Excel Tables), formulas that follow your edits, a heat map that is
conditional formatting, and real PivotTables over the database.

Three tools, three questions — pick by the question:

| | `fetch` | `research` (this) | `cycle` (Postgres pipeline) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Answers | "what is on this store's shelf?" | "what do the markets charge for *my* list?" | "what is the canonical price of everything, continuously?" |
| Input | a source + category or query | a product list (xlsx / csv / txt) | none (sweeps everything) |
| Output | JSON lines | `.xlsx` + `research.json` | PostgreSQL |
| Needs | `requests` | `requests`, `openpyxl`; Excel optional | PostgreSQL |

## Quick start

```bash
pip install -e "narxnoma-crawler[excel]"             # adds openpyxl
cd narxnoma-crawler

python -m narxnoma.research run --targets products.xlsx --out research.xlsx
python -m narxnoma.research run --targets list.txt --category smartphones \
        --sources uzum,olcha,texnomart --out phones.xlsx
python -m narxnoma.research workbook --from research.json --out research.xlsx   # no fetching
python -m narxnoma.research check research.xlsx                                  # Excel recalculates, reports errors
```

`run` writes `research.json` next to the workbook (override with `--json`). Rebuilding a
workbook from it costs no requests, so a change to the workbook layout, the Settings
defaults or a baseline never needs a re-crawl.

### Input

| file | shape |
|---|---|
| `.xlsx` / `.xlsm` | first sheet, header row |
| `.csv` | UTF-8 (BOM ok), delimiter sniffed (`, ; tab`) |
| `.txt` | one name per line; `name;price` or `name<TAB>price`; `#` comments |

Columns are found by header name, case-insensitively (`targets.ALIASES`): **name**
(`Product`, `Product Name`, `Товар`, `Наименование`, `Mahsulot`…), **price** (`Our price`,
`Наша цена`, `Narx`…), **category** (`Category`, `Категория`…), **currency** (`UZS` default,
`USD` converted at the central-bank rate fetched live, or `--usd-rate`), **note**. *Any other
column is kept* as an extra column on `Products` — your own brand / supplier / segment
columns become pivot dimensions for free.

Only the name is required. With no price the baseline falls back to the market median.
A category is a narxnoma slug (`smartphones televisions laptops acs fridges washers ovens`);
`targets.CATEGORY_ALIASES` maps RU/UZ/EN words to it. A product with no category (and no
`--category`) is **not matched** — the matcher's identity model depends on it — and is
reported.

## The pipeline

1. **Collect** (`collect.py`). Every sweepable source is swept once per category present in
   the list and cached for 24 h in `database/research_cache/<source>__<category>.json`
   (`--max-age-h`, `--force`). Sweeping is the point: it gives every product the same
   candidate pool and lets a price be **re-matched without re-fetching**. A failed sweep
   raises inside the source and is **caught per (source, category)**: it is written to that
   market's Note, the market's `fetched_at` stays empty, the run continues. A half-swept
   catalogue is never recorded as a success, and a cache file is written atomically so a
   crash cannot leave a truncated one. Search-only sources (`mediapark`) get one query per
   product, nearly verbatim (only the leading category noun is dropped: a ranking
   autocomplete needs the RAM/storage and colour words to rank the exact variant). **glotr
   is excluded by default** — its robots.txt forbids crawling and it is used only by grant
   at its `Crawl-delay` (`docs/parsers/glotr.md`); name it in `--sources` deliberately.
2. **Match** (`match.py`). The shared matcher, unchanged: brand, model, RAM and storage must
   match; colour is not identity except on iPhones. Targets are bucketed by brand first
   (a brand mismatch is a hard reject before anything else; unknown-brand listings are
   always considered). The accept threshold is per category: **30 for phones, 25 for
   everything else** — a television has no storage/RAM/identity-colour, so a *perfect*
   brand + bidirectional model-code match scores exactly 25, and 30 would empty the
   category. 25 is not looser: it still requires the model tokens to match in both
   directions.
3. **Keep every accepted offer.** Nothing is reduced at this layer. A marketplace with 20
   sellers contributes 20 rows; `Offers` is the database the analysis runs on.
4. **Build** (`workbook.py`), then **pivots** (`excel/`), then **verify**. In that order,
   and nothing may re-save the file through openpyxl afterwards.

### Known limits (read before quoting a result)

- The matcher is used as-is. Eight gaps that openshop's names exposed are documented, not
  fixed (`docs/parsers/openshop.md` §8): e.g. `iPhone 16 128GB (SIM + eSIM)` and
  `Samsung 55" Crystal UHD DU7100 ... (UE55DU7100UXUZ)` are rejected as the same product.
  Precision over recall: a miss shows as an empty cell, never as a wrong price. Fixing a
  gap needs a golden-corpus measurement first (`narxnoma-seed/golden/`).
- `Name delta` on `Offers` lists words present in one name but not the other. Non-empty is
  not an error; it is the row to read before quoting.
- A listing at an implausible price (outside 100 000 – 100 000 000 UZS) is dropped by the
  source client, so very cheap accessories and very expensive display units never appear.
- Categories outside the seven narxnoma knows are not matched.

## The workbook

| Sheet | What it is | Edit it? |
|---|---|---|
| `Start` | index with links, how to use | — |
| `Prices` | product × market grid: the **cheapest accepted offer per market**, as formulas over `tblOffers`; plus Baseline, Markets matched, min, median, Δ ours vs median, cheapest market. **The heat map.** | no — formulas |
| `Heatmap` | the same grid as % difference from the baseline, graded colour scale | no |
| `Pivot …` | PivotTables over `tblOffers` (see below) | drag fields freely |
| `Offers` | Excel Table **`tblOffers`** — one row per offer, every one kept. Pivot source. | add / delete rows |
| `Products` | Excel Table **`tblProducts`** — one row per product. **Yellow cells are inputs.** | edit Our price, Category |
| `Markets` | Excel Table **`tblMarkets`** — coverage, average delta, share cheaper than baseline, cheapest wins, freshness, the reason a market is empty; bar chart | no |
| `Links` | as-fetched snapshot, each price a hyperlink to its offer | no |
| `Settings` | `cfg_par` `cfg_mid` `cfg_sat` thresholds and `cfg_baseline` (`Our price` / `Market median`) | **yes** |
| `Notes` | definitions, per-market freshness, what an empty cell means | — |

### How edits propagate

`Products[Our price]` → `Products[Baseline]` → `Offers[Baseline]` → `Offers[Delta vs baseline]`
→ `Offers[Band]`, and → `Prices` baseline → heat-map colours, `Heatmap`, `Markets`.
Change a price of yours, flip `cfg_baseline` to `Market median`, tighten a threshold, or
**append a row to `tblOffers`** (the calculated columns fill themselves, the grid picks the
offer up): everything follows. This was verified in real Excel (see *Verification*).

### Definitions

- **Baseline** = your price if you gave one and `cfg_baseline` is `Our price`; otherwise the
  market median.
- **Market median** = median of one representative (cheapest) price per market, so a
  marketplace with many sellers does not outvote a single-shop retailer.
- **Delta vs baseline** = (market price − baseline) / baseline. **Negative = the market is
  cheaper than you.** (`Delta ours vs median` on `Prices` is the other way round:
  (our − median) / median, positive = you are dearer.)
- **Bands** (`Offers[Band]`, sort-prefixed so a pivot orders them): `1 Market much cheaper`
  ≤ −`cfg_sat` · `2 Market cheaper` ≤ −`cfg_mid` · `3 Market slightly cheaper` < −`cfg_par` ·
  `4 About equal` within ±`cfg_par` · `5…7` mirror image on the dear side. Defaults 2 % /
  10 % / 20 %.
- An **empty cell** means "no match found", never a price of zero.

### The heat map

Conditional formatting, never a baked fill. On `Prices`, seven rules over the market
block: a bold dark border on the cheapest price in each row, and six graded fills (three red
for a market cheaper than the baseline, three green for dearer). Each rule compares a cell
with **its own row's baseline** (`$N2`, absolute column, relative row) and reads its
thresholds from the named Settings cells. That is the one thing a built-in colour scale cannot
do (its endpoints come from the range, not from a per-row baseline) and a formula rule can.
`Heatmap` uses a real 3-colour scale whose end points are `-cfg_sat` / `0` / `+cfg_sat`.

Why this matters: a painted fill goes stale the moment a price is edited, and a wrong colour
is read faster than the number it contradicts. These cannot go stale, and they survive a
re-sort and a filter.

## Pivots — three tiers

| `--pivots` | Needs | Gives |
|---|---|---|
| `com` (auto when Excel is present) | Windows + desktop Excel | three PivotTables on one shared cache (Category×Market avg delta with a pivot-scoped colour scale; Product×Market cheapest price; Market×Band counts), **three slicers** (Category, Market, Band) that drive all of them, a **pivot chart**, neutral captions (no localised "Row Labels"), and — if this Excel has Power Pivot — a **Data Model** (below) |
| `ooxml` (auto when no Excel) | nothing | the same three PivotTables, written into the .xlsx package with `refreshOnLoad`; Excel builds them when the file is opened. No slicers, chart, Data Model; built-in number formats only |
| `none` | nothing | Tables + formulas + heat map; Insert > PivotTable > `tblOffers` does the rest |

### Data Model (`--model auto|yes|no`)

With `com`, the script **probes** `workbook.Model`. If present it loads `tblOffers`,
`tblProducts`, `tblMarkets` into the model, relates `Offers[Product] → Products[Product]` and
`Offers[Market] → Markets[Market]`, adds DAX measures (`Offers count`, `Median price`,
`Cheapest price`, `Avg delta`, `Share cheaper than baseline`) and a model-based PivotTable
(`Pivot model (DAX)`). If the probe fails — no Power Pivot, or any error — it prints
`MODEL unavailable: <reason>` and the workbook keeps the flat pivots: **flat is the fallback,
nothing is lost** (`--model yes` fails loudly instead). Both are present when the model works,
so you can compare.

## Verification

`python -m narxnoma.research check book.xlsx` opens the file in real Excel, recalculates
fully, and prints one line per sheet: formula-error cells, tables, pivots, conditional-format
rule counts, slicers. `run` / `workbook` call it automatically when Excel is present and exit 1
on any error cell. Why: openpyxl writes formulas it cannot evaluate, so a `#NAME?` is invisible
until a person opens the file.

What was verified on 2026-10-22 (real Excel 16, 25 synthetic products × 10 markets, 230
offers, and a live 8-product × 4-market run):

- 0 formula-error cells on all sheets; the file reopens with the right counts of tables,
  pivots, slicers.
- Every `Prices` cell, median, baseline, `Delta vs baseline`, cheapest flag, band and every
  `Markets` figure matched an **independent Python recomputation** (0 mismatches).
- Pivot and DAX figures were **spot-checked** against the same recomputation (category ×
  market averages, a market's grand total, offer counts, share cheaper than baseline, a band
  count); the whole pivot body was not compared cell by cell.
- Edits propagate: our price, the `Market median` switch, the thresholds and an appended
  `tblOffers` row each changed the displayed colours / bands as expected.
- The raw-OOXML pivots open and populate on open, with no manual refresh.

`pytest -m excel tests/research` repeats the Excel-side checks on any machine that has Excel;
the default `pytest` run skips them.

## Requirements

- Python ≥ 3.11, `requests`, `openpyxl ≥ 3.1` (`[excel]` extra).
- `MINIFS` / `MAXIFS` (used by `Prices`, `Products`, `Offers`) need **Excel 2019 / Microsoft
  365** or LibreOffice. They are written with the `_xlfn.` prefix; without it Excel shows
  `#NAME?` until a cell is re-entered.
- COM tier: Windows, desktop Excel, PowerShell. Data Model: an Excel with Power Pivot.
- Without Excel the formulas still work when a person opens the file, but cached values are
  absent, so a previewer or pandas shows blanks. Run `check --save` (needs Excel) to bake the
  calculated values in.

## Where things are

| File | Role |
|---|---|
| `research/targets.py` | read the product list; alias tables |
| `research/collect.py` | sweep + cache, search-only, market order, failure notes |
| `research/match.py` | brand-bucketed matching, category thresholds, name delta |
| `research/model.py` | `Target` / `Offer` / `Research`, JSON round-trip |
| `research/run.py` | `run_research`: collect → match; CBU USD rate |
| `research/workbook.py` | the workbook (openpyxl): tables, formulas, heat map, Settings, Notes |
| `research/build.py` | `make_workbook`: workbook → pivots → verify |
| `research/excel/pivots.ps1` / `pivots.py` | COM: pivots, slicers, chart, Data Model probe |
| `research/excel/ooxml_pivot.py` | raw-OOXML pivots, no Excel |
| `research/excel/verify.ps1` | Excel recalculates and reports errors, tables, pivots, fills |
| `tests/research/` | offline tests; `-m excel` for the Excel-side ones |

FILE: docs/parsers/README.md

# Price-source API reference

Rebuild-grade notes for every site this project fetches from. Each file documents
the **exact request**, the **response JSON shape**, **which field is the price and in
what unit**, how to build a **valid product URL**, and the **traps that silently
produce wrong numbers**.

Everything here was derived from live traffic (browser Network tab) and then
verified by running the parser end to end and opening the resulting links.

> **Verified 2026-07-26.** Each retailer client was re-implemented from scratch using
> only what these files state, and every documented endpoint, field name, unit and URL
> pattern was asserted live — **48/48 checks passed**. That pass also corrected two
> real errors in the uzum notes (see §1 and §2 there): `Origin` is *not* the required
> header for minting a token — `Accept-Language` and `Referer` are — and the GraphQL
> endpoint needs `apollographql-client-name` + `x-iid`, without which it returns
> **HTTP 200 with an `errors` key rather than an HTTP error**.
>
> (The two internal systems were checked in that pass too; they are out of scope here —
> see the aggregator note above.)

> **Second pass, 2026-07-26 — matching quality.** The first pass verified *transport*:
> endpoints, fields, units, URLs. It could not catch matcher defects, because those
> return plausible numbers. Benchmarking all seven clients against 20 externally-sourced
> targets found three more errors, now fixed in place:
>
> 1. **[_matching-engine.md](_matching-engine.md)** — a bare `"16 256"` was read as
>    RAM = 16, which deleted the model number from `model_tokens` and made
>    **iPhone 16, 17 and Air mutually interchangeable** (measured: a 12,000,000
>    iPhone 16 matched an iPhone Air target, 1.44 M low). `air`/`fe`/`se` were also
>    missing from `MODEL_MODIFIERS`.
> 2. **[uzum.md §8](uzum.md#8-colours-are-russian--and-in-cardmodel-strings-uzbek)** —
>    "colours are Russian only" is false for card/model strings, which are often Uzbek.
>    Adding `Kumush`/`Tilla`/`Yashil`/`Muz` took uzum from **5/20 to 15/20**.
> 3. **[alifshop.md §4](alifshop.md#4-query-building--two-shot-colour-stripped-first)** —
>    "broad stripping isn't needed" is false; a `Glacier Blue` query never reaches the
>    `Синий` listing. Now two-shot.
>
> Lesson worth generalising: **a transport check cannot validate a matcher.** Wrong
> matches look exactly like right ones until you diff against an independent source.

## Files

| File | Site | Kind | Auth | Coverage* |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [uzum.md](uzum.md) | uzum.uz | GraphQL, multi-seller | anonymous token (self-minting) | 61/66 |
| [alifshop.md](alifshop.md) | alifshop.uz | REST POST, multi-seller | none | 63/66 |
| [beemarket.md](beemarket.md) | market.beeline.uz | REST GET, single card | none | 57/66 |
| [idea.md](idea.md) | idea.uz | REST GET, single card | none | 54/66 |
| [olcha.md](olcha.md) | olcha.uz | REST GET, full catalogue | none | 52/66 |
| [texnomart.md](texnomart.md) | texnomart.uz | REST GET, single card | none | 50/66 |
| [mediapark.md](mediapark.md) | mediapark.uz | REST GET, autocomplete | none | 46/66 |
| [asaxiy.md](asaxiy.md) | asaxiy.uz | **server-rendered HTML** | none | — † |
| [radius.md](radius.md) | radius.uz | Saleor GraphQL, single card | none | — † |
| [glotr.md](glotr.md) | glotr.uz | **server-rendered HTML**, classifieds board | none (by grant — see the file) | — † |
| [openshop.md](openshop.md) | openshop.uz | REST POST, full catalogue (~120 requests) | none | — ‡ |
| [_matching-engine.md](_matching-engine.md) | — | shared name→product matcher | — | — |

> **Narxnoma is an aggregator.** The two internal systems documented in the original
> notes — `ecom.ishonchsavdo.uz` (our storefront) and `ishonchsale.uz` (our warehouse) —
> are deliberately **excluded** from this repo. We never list our own inventory beside
> competitors' prices. Their notes remain in the source `parsers/` directory.

\* phones matched out of 66 active smartphones, as of 2026-07-24.

† asaxiy (2026-08-10), radius and glotr (2026-08-11) were added after that
survey, so they have no 66-phone score. Measured on their own terms: asaxiy 693
offers at 96.8% attached; radius 274 offers at 98.9%; glotr 8,138 offers.

‡ openshop (2026-10-14) came after all three. Measured on its own terms on
2026-10-22: 769 offers across the seven categories, 5,723 products in the whole
catalogue, every one in stock.

> **Fourth pass, 2026-10-14 — a whole assortment against one new store.**
> openshop was first used to price a retailer's *entire* warehouse list (2,619
> names across 118 product types, most of them not phones) against one catalogue,
> and every matched name was then read by hand. Every automated check passed while
> the names were wrong, and every defect was in the **caller's** use of the matcher
> on names it had never been measured on. None is fixed in `matching/` yet — its
> golden corpus pins the phone behaviour — so callers outside phones must handle
> them; the repros are in [openshop.md §8](openshop.md#8-matcher-gaps-its-names-exposed):
>
> 1. `"(SIM + eSIM)"` written **with spaces** became a phantom `plus` model token,
>    so **every iPhone** was rejected (0 matched; 16 once worked around).
> 2. The non-phone path drops one-character tokens, model digits included:
>    **Galaxy Watch 4 matched Watch 8, Buds 2 Pro matched Buds 4 Pro.**
> 3. `head_for_model` cuts an appliance name at `/`, so **PRM-20MW/LA1, /LA3 and
>    /LA4 all matched /LA2** at one price.
>
> Lesson, alongside the earlier passes': **the matcher is only as tested as the
> names it has seen.** A new store's naming style is a new test corpus; read the
> pairs before trusting the counts.

> **Third pass, 2026-08-11 — what is on the row, not how many rows.** The first
> pass verified transport and the second verified matching. Neither could see a
> field that is *populated, well-formed, and wrong*, because every count stays
> identical. Three defects of exactly that shape, all now fixed:
>
> 1. **[glotr.md §3](glotr.md#3-trap--the-photos-come-before-the-name)** — the
>    photos sit *before* the name inside a card, so cutting cards at the name
>    anchor gave every listing its neighbour's picture. **55 of 55 wrong** on
>    the page it was measured on; row counts, attachment and the coverage
>    report were all unchanged by it.
> 2. **[glotr.md §4](glotr.md#4-trap--past-its-last-page-glotr-serves-other-peoples-goods)** —
>    past its last page glotr serves 21 *unrelated* products per page,
>    endlessly and never repeating, so the no-new-ids stop never fired. A
>    category holding 240 ovens ingested 2,739.
> 3. **[uzum.md](uzum.md)** — `photos` was never requested, so the largest
>    source in the catalogue published **5,461 offers with no picture at all**.
>
> Lesson worth generalising, alongside the second pass's: **aggregate metrics
> cannot see a substitution.** Count, freshness and attachment answer "is there
> a value here?", never "is it this product's value?". Checking that needs a
> second, independently-rendered copy of the same fact — the `-p-<id>` in the
> photo anchor, the `alt` text, the site's own pager — and a rule that drops
> the row when the two disagree.

> **A small source is not a weak source.** radius contributes 2.2% of the
> catalogue's offers and 4.8% of its *comparisons* — 212 of its products are
> carried by someone else, and 54 of those had only one price before it
> arrived. Judge a candidate source by the second prices it creates, not by
> its row count. See [radius.md §8](radius.md#8-measured-result).

## The one thing to understand first

Almost none of these sites can be scraped from HTML — nearly every one is a
JavaScript SPA that renders an empty shell. A headless-browser attempt across five
of them succeeded on exactly one. **The durable path is usually the private JSON API
the SPA itself calls.**

To find it for a new site: open the site, DevTools → Network → Fetch/XHR, type a
search, and look for the request whose response contains prices. Copy it as cURL.

**Check for the exception before assuming the rule.** asaxiy.uz has no catalogue
JSON endpoint at all — its only `/ajax/*` routes are login and filter plumbing —
and it server-renders a complete, fully-priced product grid into the initial HTML
document. Looking for an API there finds nothing and concludes, wrongly, that the
site cannot be crawled. See [asaxiy.md](asaxiy.md), including why the identity is
still read from machine-generated `data-` attributes rather than from prose, and
why the lighter XHR fragment is the wrong thing to fetch.

## Two shapes of retailer

This distinction decides how you turn results into one number:

- **Single-card** (beemarket, texnomart, idea, mediapark, olcha, openshop) — the retailer is
  the only seller, so one variant appears once. Take the best exact match's price.
- **Multi-seller** (uzum, alifshop) — many partners list the same variant at
  different prices. Collect all matches, strip outliers with IQR fences, take the
  **median**. uzum additionally reports a **min**.

```python
import statistics

def robust_median(prices):
    """Median after IQR outlier stripping (only strips when >= 4 samples)."""
    prices = sorted(prices)
    if not prices:
        return None, []
    if len(prices) < 4:
        return statistics.median(prices), prices
    q1, _, q3 = statistics.quantiles(prices, n=4, method="inclusive")
    iqr = q3 - q1
    lo, hi = q1 - 1.5 * iqr, q3 + 1.5 * iqr
    kept = [p for p in prices if lo <= p <= hi] or prices
    return statistics.median(kept), kept
```

## Rules that apply to every source

1. **Never trust a slug you built yourself.** Two sites punished this. alifshop
   products live at `/moderated-offer/<slug>`, not `/product/<slug>` (404).
   mediapark's slug is actively wrong — a 256 GB Silver phone carries a slug reading
   `...8-128-gb...`. Always open one built URL in a real browser before trusting the
   column.
2. **Verify the price against the rendered page**, not just against the JSON. This
   is how the uzum card-vs-SKU bug was caught.
3. **Clear the cell on a miss.** If a filler ever writes only on success, a later
   stricter run leaves stale values from an earlier looser pass behind. This
   produced a real data-integrity bug (idea column showed 60 filled when only 54
   matched). Every filler now writes `None` to both the price and link cell when
   there is no match.
4. **Search relevance is not precision.** Every endpoint here will happily return a
   phone case, a vacuum cleaner, or a different storage tier. The matcher — not the
   search engine — decides what counts. See [_matching-engine.md](_matching-engine.md).
5. **Broad query, strict match.** Several of these engines use strict AND semantics,
   so extra words (colour, `5G`, brand prefix) silently return zero results. Strip
   them from the *query* and enforce them in the *matcher* instead.
6. Be polite: sequential requests, ~0.3–0.6 s delay, a real `User-Agent`, and the
   `Origin`/`Referer` the site's own SPA sends.

## Output contract used by the fillers

All fillers edit `exports/<date>_phone_prices_survey.xlsx` in place and touch only
their own columns:

- Sheet **`Prices`** — integer SUM, `number_format = "#,##0"`
- Sheet **`Links`** — plain-text URL, `hyperlink = None`, `number_format = "@"`
  (kept unlinked deliberately so the cells stay copy-pasteable)

Each run also writes a full audit trail to `database/<site>_price_fill_log.json`
containing every candidate considered, its score, and the matched name — which is
what makes "why did this row get this price?" answerable after the fact.



================================================
FILE: docs/parsers/_matching-engine.md
================================================
# The shared matching engine

Every filler uses the same name→product matcher. **This, not the search endpoint, is
what makes results correct.** Each site's search will happily return a phone case, a
different storage tier, or a different colour; the matcher is the only thing that
decides what counts.

Design rule: **broad query, strict match.** Widen recall at the HTTP layer, restore
precision here.

---

## 1. Normalisation

```python
def normalize(s):
    if not s:
        return ""
    t = str(s).lower().strip()
    for a, b in [("–", "-"), ("—", "-"), (",", " "), ("/", " "), ("|", " "), ("ё", "е")]:
        t = t.replace(a, b)
    # '+' → ' plus ' ONLY as a trailing model marker (e.g. "Pro+"). A '+' inside
    # "SIM+eSIM" is followed by a letter, so it becomes a plain space instead.
    t = re.sub(r"\+(?=\s|$)", " plus ", t)
    t = t.replace("+", " ")
    t = re.sub(r"[^\w\s]", " ", t, flags=re.UNICODE)
    t = re.sub(r"\s+", " ", t).strip()
    return t
```

The `+` rule exists because of a real failure: mapping every `+` to `plus` turned
`SIM+eSIM` into a phantom `plus` model token that rejected **every iPhone** on
BeeMarket. `ё → е` matters for uzum, where `Тёмно-зелёный` and `Темно-зеленый` both
appear.

## 2. Feature extraction

```python
def extract_features(name):
    n = normalize(name)
    n = re.sub(r"\bсмартфон\b", "", n).strip()
    n = re.sub(r"\bsmartfon\b", "", n).strip()

    brand = None
    for b in ["iphone", "apple", "samsung", "honor", "xiaomi", "redmi", "vivo",
              "oppo", "realme", "tecno", "infinix", "poco", "nothing", "google", "asus"]:
        if b in n:
            brand = {"iphone": "apple", "redmi": "xiaomi"}.get(b, b)
            break

    storage = ram = None

    # Collect ALL unit-bearing figures — sellers write "8ГБ/256ГБ" and "12GB+512GB",
    # where a single-match regex reads the RAM as the storage.
    units = []
    for m in re.finditer(r"(?<![a-zа-я\d])(\d{1,4})\s*(gb|гб|tb|тб)\b", n):
        val = int(m.group(1))
        if m.group(2) in ("tb", "тб"):
            val *= 1024
        units.append(val)

    if len(units) >= 2:
        storage = max(units)
        if min(units) <= 24:
            ram = min(units)
    elif len(units) == 1:
        if units[0] >= 32: storage = units[0]
        else:              ram = units[0]

    # Plausible RAM/storage sizes for the bare-pair fallbacks below. A model
    # number ("7" in "Galaxy Z Fold 7 512 ГБ", "5" in "Galaxy Watch 5 44mm")
    # has the identical shape as a real memory pair; restricting candidates
    # to sizes retailers actually sell is what stops the model number from
    # being absorbed as RAM or storage. See "The phantom-RAM trap" below.
    PLAUSIBLE_RAM_GB = {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 16, 18, 24}
    PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB = {32, 64, 128, 256, 512, 1024, 2048}

    # Both pair regexes are zero-width: the two figures are captured inside a
    # lookahead instead of being consumed, so re.finditer's matches can overlap.
    # A model number directly adjacent to RAM ("Redmi Note 15 12/256" normalises
    # to "redmi note 15 12 256") would otherwise make the model+RAM pair
    # ("15", "12") consume "12" and hide the real memory pair ("12", "256") from
    # ever being scanned. See "The phantom-RAM trap" below.
    if storage is None:                       # bare "6 128" form
        for m in re.finditer(r"(?<![a-zа-я\d])(?=(\d{1,3})\s+(\d{2,4})(?!\d))", n):
            a, b = int(m.group(1)), int(m.group(2))
            if max(a, b) not in PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB:
                continue
            storage = max(a, b); break

    # LONE figure: a capacity with neither a unit nor a partner. Every rule
    # above needs one or the other, so "Apple iPhone 12 pro 128 Синий" read as
    # no memory at all and the listing was unkeyable — while
    # "Apple iPhone 12 128 Алый" read 128 correctly, purely because the model
    # number happened to sit beside the capacity. Common rather than exotic:
    # uzum states memory in a per-SKU characteristic whose value is often the
    # bare figure (Память: "128"), which reaches the name with nothing beside
    # it. A lone figure is weaker evidence than a pair, so it is admitted only
    # from PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB and only when NOT followed by a unit marking it
    # as something else — 64 (МП), 128 (mp) and 512 all appear in blurbs.
    if storage is None:                       # lone "128" form
        for m in re.finditer(r"(?<![a-zа-я\d])(\d{2,4})(?!\d)", n):
            val = int(m.group(1))
            if val not in PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB:
                continue
            if re.match(r"^\s*(mp|мп|mah|мач|hz|гц|mm|мм|w|вт|nm|нм|dpi|ppi|fps)\b",
                        n[m.end():]):
                continue
            storage = val; break

    # A bare "12 256" pair normally means RAM/storage. "iPhone 16 256 GB" has the
    # identical shape — see "The phantom-RAM trap" below — so require a real
    # separator in the RAW string, and never infer RAM for Apple.
    has_pair_sep = bool(re.search(r"\d\s*[/\\+|]\s*\d", str(name or "")))
    if ram is None and (has_pair_sep or brand != "apple"):
        for m in re.finditer(r"(?<![a-zа-я\d])(?=(\d{1,2})\s+(\d{2,4})(?!\d))", n):
            a, b = int(m.group(1)), int(m.group(2))
            if min(a, b) not in PLAUSIBLE_RAM_GB or max(a, b) not in PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB:
                continue
            ram = min(a, b); break

    # GLOBAL Apple-RAM suppression — see "Apple RAM suppression is global" below.
    # This runs after EVERY path, not just the bare-pair fallback above.
    stated_ram = ram                      # what the retailer actually wrote
    if brand == "apple":
        ram = None

    tokens = set(n.split())
    colors = {COLOR_MAP[t] for t in tokens if t in COLOR_MAP}

    # stated_ram is reserved as well as ram: a SUPPRESSED memory figure is
    # still a memory figure and must not fall through into model_tokens.
    reserved_nums = {str(storage or ""), str(ram or ""), str(stated_ram or ""),
                     "5g", "4g", "0"}
    model_tokens = set()
    for t in tokens:
        if t in NON_MODEL_TOKENS or t in COLOR_MAP or t in reserved_nums:
            continue
        if re.fullmatch(r"\d+(?:gb|гб|tb|тб)", t):
            continue
        # Skip SKU/catalogue codes (letters+digits, len >= 5): "a075f", "sm-a175f".
        # Real model designators (a07, a17, x9d, y31d) are <= 4 chars.
        if len(t) >= 5 and any(c.isalpha() for c in t) and any(c.isdigit() for c in t):
            continue
        if any(c.isdigit() for c in t):
            if t.isdigit() and int(t) in {(storage or -1), (ram or -1),
                                          (stated_ram or -1)}:
                continue
            model_tokens.add(t)
        elif t in MODEL_MODIFIERS:
            model_tokens.add(t)

    return {"brand": brand, "ram": ram, "storage": storage,
            "colors": colors, "n": n, "model_tokens": model_tokens}
```

### The lookbehind that matters

`(?<![a-zа-я\d])` before the first number. Without it, `"Samsung Galaxy A17 128GB"`
parses as the pair `"17 128"` → **ram = 17**. The model code must not be adjacent-read
as memory.

### The phantom-RAM trap that merges Apple models

The single worst bug found so far, because it fails *silently and expensively*.

```
"Apple iPhone 16 256 GB Black"   → units = [256] → storage = 256, ram = None
                                 → bare-pair loop sees "16 256" → ram = 16   ← WRONG
```

`16` is now in `reserved_nums`, so it is **stripped from `model_tokens`**. The same
happens to `iPhone 17`. Both end up with *no model token at all*, and since the
reverse check has nothing to compare, **iPhone 16, iPhone 17 and iPhone Air all match
each other**. Measured live: an `iPhone Air 256 Space Black` target matched
`Apple iPhone 16 256 GB Black` at 12,000,000 — 1.44 M below the true Air price.

Two independent causes, both must be fixed:

1. **`normalize()` has already erased the `/`** that distinguishes `12/256` (memory)
   from `16 256` (model + memory). By the time the loop runs the shapes are
   identical — so test the **raw** string for a separator, not the normalised one.
2. **`"air"` was missing from `MODEL_MODIFIERS`**, so an `iPhone Air` target carried
   no model token even before the RAM bug. `fe` (Galaxy S25 FE) and `se` are the same
   class of genuine designator.

Apple never publishes RAM in consumer names, which is what makes
`has_pair_sep or brand != "apple"` safe: it keeps `Honor X9d 12/256` (real separator)
and `Samsung A17 6 128` (non-Apple bare form) working untouched.

> Regression test worth keeping: `iPhone 17 Pro` must reject `iPhone 17 Pro Max`,
> `iPhone 16 Pro` **and** `iPhone Air`; `Honor X9d 12/256` must still reject `8/256`.

#### The same trap recurs on Samsung — and the Apple guard doesn't cover it

`has_pair_sep or brand != "apple"` only protects brands that never publish RAM. Samsung
*does* publish RAM, so it is exempt from the guard — and it hits the identical failure
shape, because the Galaxy Z line uses a small integer as its model/generation number:

```
"Смартфон Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 7 512 ГБ Jet Black"  → bare pair "7 512" → ram = 7  ← WRONG
"Смартфон Samsung Galaxy Z Flip 7 256 ГБ Jet Black"  → bare pair "7 256" → ram = 7  ← WRONG
"Samsung Galaxy Watch 5 44mm Graphite"               → bare pair "5 44"  → ram = 5, storage = 44  ← WRONG
```

`7` then lands in `reserved_nums`, deleting the model number from `model_tokens` —
exactly the mechanism that merged `iPhone 16`/`17`/`Air`, this time merging `Z Fold 7`
and `Z Fold 6`. Checked against the project's 2,834-name corpus this misreads 13
listings, including current flagship phones.

Brand exclusion can't be the fix here (Samsung genuinely does publish RAM in most of
its listings, so blanket-excluding it would break the working cases). The general
defence is **plausibility**: constrain both bare-pair passes to values retailers
actually sell, and keep scanning past a rejected candidate instead of stopping at the
first one —

```python
PLAUSIBLE_RAM_GB = {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 16, 18, 24}
PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB = {32, 64, 128, 256, 512, 1024, 2048}
```

The bare-pair storage loop now requires `max(a, b) in PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB`; the
bare-pair RAM loop requires `min(a, b) in PLAUSIBLE_RAM_GB and max(a, b) in
PLAUSIBLE_STORAGE_GB`. Either way, on a rejected candidate the loop `continue`s to the
next regex match instead of `break`ing, which is what lets `"... 4/128 ..."` still be
found in a name whose marketing blurb also contains an unrelated `"9 120"`-shaped pair
(`6.9"` + `120Hz`). The unit-bearing pass (`8ГБ`/`256GB`) is untouched — an explicit
unit is a statement by the retailer and stays trusted as-is. The Apple guard also stays
exactly as it is; this is additional, not a replacement — Apple's case (no RAM
published at all) is still handled by `brand != "apple"`, while Samsung's case (RAM
published, but a plausible-looking model number sits right next to storage) is handled
by the plausibility sets.

Plausibility alone isn't enough, though — `continue` only helps if there is a next
candidate *to* continue to. A two-digit model number sitting directly in front of a
two-digit RAM value (`"Redmi Note 15 12/256"` normalises to `"redmi note 15 12 256"`)
has the same non-overlapping problem the lookbehind was built to avoid: a plain
consuming regex finds `"15 12"` first, rejects it on plausibility, and then has already
consumed the `"12"` — so the real pair `"12 256"` is never offered as a candidate at
all, and `ram` comes back `None` even though scanning "past" the rejected pair looks
like it should have worked. That's why both pair regexes are zero-width (the two
figures live inside a lookahead, matched but not consumed): `re.finditer` can then
return overlapping candidates, so after `"15 12"` is rejected, `"12 256"` is still
found starting one character later.

#### Apple RAM suppression is global, not just on the bare-pair path

`has_pair_sep or brand != "apple"` guards **only the bare-pair fallback**. The
unit-bearing pass above it has no guard at all, so an Apple listing that spells the
units out leaks RAM straight through:

```
"iPhone 17 Pro 12/256GB Silver"  → units = [12, 256] → ram = 12    ← WRONG
"iPhone 17 Pro 256 ГБ, Silver"   → units = [256]     → ram = None
```

Same phone, two canonical products — `apple-17-pro-12-256-silver` and
`apple-17-pro-256-silver` — so the offer pool splits in two and the site shows two
different prices for one product. 47 listings in the corpus are affected. Note this is
the *opposite* failure to the phantom-RAM trap above: that one **merged** distinct
models, this one **splits** a single one. Both come from the same ambiguity.

The fix is one suppression at the end of memory extraction, covering every path:

```python
if brand in BRANDS_WITHOUT_PUBLISHED_RAM:
    ram = None
```

Apple does not publish RAM. Any RAM figure in an Apple listing therefore came from a
**reseller's own wording**, not from the manufacturer, and must never become identity —
regardless of how confidently it is written (`12/256GB`, `12 GB RAM`, a separator, a
unit). The exemption for an explicit separator is gone: a separator proves the seller
*meant* a memory pair, which was never the question.

> **Suppressing RAM is not enough on its own.** `reserved_nums` is built from `ram`, so
> zeroing `ram` at the return boundary lets the figure fall through into
> `model_tokens`: `"iPhone 17 Pro 12/256GB"` keeps `"12"` as a *model* token and keys as
> `apple-12-17-pro-256-silver`. The split survives in a new place, and the reverse
> model-token check now hard-rejects the listing outright — strictly worse than the bug
> being fixed. Memory extraction must therefore return the pre-suppression figure
> (`stated_ram`) alongside, and feature extraction must reserve it.

### Why `units` collects everything

`"8ГБ/256ГБ"` normalises to `"8гб 256гб"`. A `re.search` for the *first* unit match
returns 8 → storage = 8 → every such listing rejected. On uzum this silently killed
all Honor 600 matches until fixed.

## 3. Vocabularies

```python
MODEL_MODIFIERS = {"pro", "ultra", "lite", "plus", "max", "mini", "note", "redmi",
                   "air", "fe", "se", "fold", "flip"}

NON_MODEL_TOKENS = {"gb", "гб", "tb", "тб", "smartfon", "смартфон", "iphone",
                    "apple", "samsung", "honor", "xiaomi", "vivo", "oppo",
                    "realme", "tecno", "infinix", "poco", "nothing", "google",
                    "asus", "esim", "nanosim", "sim", "nano", "point"}
```

**`"galaxy"` is deliberately absent** from `MODEL_MODIFIERS`. mediapark lists the A07
as `"SAMSUNG SM-A075F A07"` with no "Galaxy" — requiring it rejects valid matches.
Model identity comes from the `a07`/`a17`/`s26` token instead.

#### A dropped family word merges two products

The loop keeps a token only if it is digit-bearing *or* in `MODEL_MODIFIERS`. Everything
else is **silently discarded** — which is fine for `galaxy` and `z`, and catastrophic
for `fold` and `flip`:

```
"Galaxy Z Fold 7 Black 12/256"  → model_tokens = {"7"}
"Galaxy Z Flip 7 Black 12/256"  → model_tokens = {"7"}
```

Identical `model_tokens`, identical key `samsung-7-12-256-black`, `score_match` = 60,
`why()` = `"OK"`. A Fold costs roughly **double** a Flip, so the merged product shows
one median drawn from two price populations. Adding `fold` and `flip` to
`MODEL_MODIFIERS` is the whole fix.

They also join `_MODEL_PREFIX_ORDER` (with `redmi` and `note`), because they are family
words rather than trailing designators: the real name is `Galaxy Z Fold 7`, so
alphabetical ordering would render the display name as `Samsung 7 Fold`.

> The general rule this illustrates: **a word that distinguishes two products in the
> shop must be a model token.** When auditing, list every name that reduces to the same
> `model_tokens` and check none of them are actually different phones.

### Brand vocabulary outside phones (added 2026-08-10)

Non-phone categories use a second recognition vocabulary, `APPLIANCE_BRANDS`,
kept separate because `BRANDS` doubles as the **phone crawl frontier** — a
fridge maker added there spends a wasted query per cycle at three retailers.
It is grown by a repeatable measured pass, run three times so far:

> Count the unrecognised head token across every *unresolved* non-phone title,
> then confirm each candidate against three real listings as a manufacturer
> rather than a descriptor.

The third pass (873 unresolved offers, prompted by adding asaxiy) added 21
makers and 5 aliases. Four things it taught that the first two did not:

**A brand can be in the vocabulary and still never match.** `biryusa` was
present in the *Latin* spelling and its display label was already `"Бирюса"` —
but every listing writes it in Cyrillic, and `normalize()` correctly leaves an
all-Cyrillic token alone (there is no Latin in it to fold). The two spellings
never met and 20 offers were unkeyable. **A brand that appears in a non-Latin
script needs that spelling as an alias, not just as a label.**

**Code prefixes are aliases when — and only when — some listing spells the
brand out.** `prm → premier` and `shiv → shivaki` were added because uzum
publishes both forms of the same product ("Холодильник Premier PRM-250TFDF/W",
"SHIVAKI SHIV-WM 1108 Sentaku") while idea publishes the bare code. `lrf` and
`gtw` look identical in shape and were **not** added: no listing anywhere names
their maker, and guessing a brand is how two different products become one page.

**Short brands must not use the trailing-digit boundary.** The non-phone probe
matches `\b<brand>(?=\b|\d)`, where the digit allowance exists so idea's and
olcha's glued `"Hp15-fd00"` is recognised. Adding two-character `bq` for BQ's
televisions relabelled two **ASUS** laptops — `X1502VA-BQ827` is ASUS's own
SKU suffix, `normalize()` splits the hyphen to `bq827`, and the digit counted
as a boundary. Both machines became a BQ and merged with each other. `bq` now
requires a strict `\bbq\b`, which still finds the televisions.

That regression is the point of the workflow, not an aside: it was invisible to
every aggregate — attachment rose, a two-store comparison appeared, the
unresolved rate fell. **It was found only by reading every multi-store variant
the change produced, offer by offer.** See the README's standing lesson; this
is its sharper form: *aggregate metrics cannot see a substitution.*

### A CPU name is a specification on a computer and a model name elsewhere

`PROCESSORS` (intel, ryzen, arc, vega, …) is a stoplist, and `cut_at_processor`
additionally truncates the title *positionally* from the first such word — a
laptop's name ends where its spec begins.

Both are scoped to `CATEGORIES_WITH_CPU = {"laptops"}`. Measured across all
5,132 non-laptop non-phone offers, exactly 8 (0.16%) contain a `PROCESSORS`
word, and every one is an air conditioner whose product **line** collides with
a chip:

```
"Кондиционер Rulls ARC 12 Inverter"       (Intel Arc)    6 offers, 2 stores
"Кондиционер Sitronic Vega Inverter 18"   (Radeon Vega)  2 offers
```

Positional truncation made it worse than a plain stopword would: `"Rulls ARC 12"`
lost its capacity along with its name and could form no key at all. All 8 were
unresolved, so the scoping re-keyed nothing that had already clustered.

Note that half-fixing this would have been worse than leaving it: dropping
`cut_at_processor` while keeping `vega` in the stoplist yields
`"Кондиционер Sitronic 18"`, which merges every Sitronic 18 — the failure this
whole module exists to prevent.

### Colour map — base words only

```python
COLOR_MAP = {
    "black": "black", "midnight": "black",
    "white": "white",
    "silver": "silver", "meteor": "silver",
    "gray": "gray", "grey": "gray", "space": "gray",
    "gold": "gold", "desert": "gold", "sunrise": "gold",
    "blue": "blue", "cyan": "blue", "ocean": "blue",
    "deep": "blue", "navy": "blue", "sky": "blue", "glacier": "blue",
    "red": "red", "brown": "brown", "reddish": "brown",
    "green": "green", "sage": "green",
    "purple": "purple", "violet": "purple", "cobalt": "purple", "lavender": "purple",
    "orange": "orange", "cosmic": "orange", "pink": "pink",
    # ... plus the Russian AND Uzbek sets — see uzum.md §8
}
```

**Never map finish/qualifier words** — `velvet`, `light`, `golden`, `тёмно`, `светло`,
`металлик`, `меланж`. `"Velvet Grey"` → `{black, gray}` would match a **Black**
listing. The base colour word is always present anyway, so qualifiers add nothing but
false positives. This was a real bug, caught and back-ported to every site.

## 4. Scoring

```python
def score_match(t, c):          # t = target features, c = candidate features
    score = 0
    if t["brand"] and c["brand"]:
        if t["brand"] != c["brand"]:
            return -100
        score += 10
    if t["storage"] and c["storage"]:
        if t["storage"] == c["storage"]: score += 20
        else:                            return -100
    elif t["storage"] and not c["storage"]:
        return -100                      # kills accessories for free
    elif c["storage"] and not t["storage"]:
        return -100                      # symmetric — see below
    if t["ram"] and c["ram"]:
        if t["ram"] == c["ram"]: score += 10
        else:                    return -100

    # Word-boundary anchored, NOT a raw substring test — see below.
    cand_tokens = set(c["n"].split())
    for mt in t["model_tokens"]:
        if mt not in cand_tokens and not re.search(rf"\b{re.escape(mt)}\b", c["n"]):
            return -100
    for mt in c["model_tokens"]:         # reverse direction — see below
        if mt not in t["n"].split() and not re.search(rf"\b{re.escape(mt)}\b", t["n"]):
            return -100
    if t["model_tokens"] or c["model_tokens"]:
        score += 15

    if t["colors"] and c["colors"]:
        if not (t["colors"] & c["colors"]):
            return -100
        score += 5
    return score

ACCEPT_THRESHOLD = 30
```

Key properties:

- **Hard reject (`-100`) beats accumulation.** A wrong storage can never be
  out-scored by matching brand + colour.
- **Storage is symmetric; RAM is asymmetric.** This asymmetry-of-the-asymmetry is
  deliberate, and the two fields must not be treated alike:
  - Target has storage + candidate doesn't → **reject** (that's an accessory).
  - Candidate has storage + target doesn't → **reject** (see below).
  - Target has RAM + candidate doesn't → **allow**. Many retailers simply omit RAM, and
    rejecting on absence would throw away most of the market. Only a *mismatched* RAM
    rejects.
- **The reverse model-token check is essential.** It is what stops a `17 Pro Max` SKU
  from matching an `iPhone 17 Pro` target. Without it, every Pro request matches the
  more expensive Pro Max.

### A storage-less target accepts every capacity

Without the symmetric branch, a target that states no storage scores exactly

```
brand 10 + model 15 + colour 5 = 30 == ACCEPT_THRESHOLD
```

against a candidate of **any** capacity — the storage block contributes nothing and the
sum lands precisely on the threshold. Measured: `"iPhone 17 Pro Black"` accepted a
256 GB listing *and* a 1 TB listing, pooling their prices into a single median. Storage
is a hard identity field, so absence on either side must reject:

```python
elif c["storage"] and not t["storage"]:
    return -100
```

Mirror it in `why()` with a distinct message (`"target has NO storage"`) so the two
directions stay tellable apart when reading a rejection dump. RAM stays asymmetric —
absence there is genuinely "unknown", because retailers omit a RAM that exists; a
capacity, by contrast, is never omitted from a phone listing that has one.

### Model tokens must match a whole word

The fallback after the token-set test used to be a raw substring check against the
other side's normalised name. Substrings of numbers are the problem:

```
token "6"  ⊂  "iphone 16 128gb"       → iPhone 6 target accepts an iPhone 16
token "16" ⊂  "... 12 256 ..."         → any two-digit model matched a memory figure
```

Use `re.search(rf"\b{re.escape(mt)}\b", other["n"])` instead, so a token must match a
whole word. Measured against the 14,860-row golden corpus this changed **one** row: a
malformed uzum title (`"Smartfon Smartfon Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra 6 12/256GB Oq"`)
whose stray `6` previously found a home inside the target's `256`. That row was passing
by accident, which is exactly the class of accident the boundary anchor removes.

> Only apply the reverse check to a **curated** candidate string. On uzum the raw card
> title is a marketing blurb (`6.7" AMOLED 90Гц, камера 50Мп, 5000 мАч`) whose stray
> numbers become model tokens and reject everything. See
> [uzum.md §5](uzum.md#5-trap-1--card-price--product-price).

## 5. Unresolvable colours must reject, not pass

If a candidate states a colour you cannot map, dropping the colour check makes it a
**wildcard**. On uzum, `Конфетный` (candy) and `Хром` matched *both* the Awesome Gray
and Awesome Navy targets at once.

```python
if target["colors"] and cand["color_raw"] and not cand["color_known"]:
    continue        # seller stated a colour we can't resolve → can't confirm
```

Pair this with a comprehensive colour map so rejection stays rare and genuinely means
"unresolvable".

## 6. Debugging a rejection

Score alone doesn't tell you *why*. This mirror of `score_match` is worth keeping:

```python
def why(t, c):
    if t["brand"] and c["brand"] and t["brand"] != c["brand"]:
        return f"brand {c['brand']}!={t['brand']}"
    if t["storage"] and c["storage"] and t["storage"] != c["storage"]:
        return f"storage {c['storage']}!={t['storage']}"
    if t["storage"] and not c["storage"]:
        return "cand has NO storage"
    if c["storage"] and not t["storage"]:
        return "target has NO storage"
    if t["ram"] and c["ram"] and t["ram"] != c["ram"]:
        return f"ram {c['ram']}!={t['ram']}"
    ct = set(c["n"].split())
    for mt in t["model_tokens"]:
        if mt not in ct and not re.search(rf"\b{re.escape(mt)}\b", c["n"]):
            return f"target token '{mt}' missing"
    for mt in c["model_tokens"]:
        if mt not in t["n"].split() and not re.search(rf"\b{re.escape(mt)}\b", t["n"]):
            return f"cand token '{mt}' extra"
    if t["colors"] and c["colors"] and not (t["colors"] & c["colors"]):
        return f"colour {c['colors']} vs {t['colors']}"
    return "OK"
```

Every matcher fix in this project came from dumping candidates with `why()` and
reading them, not from guessing.

## 7. Validating a run

Two checks catch most regressions:

1. **min far below median → suspect.** Flag rows where `min / median < 0.88` and read
   the matched names. This is exactly how the uzum card-vs-SKU memory bug was found —
   the min was a 6/128 masquerading as an 8/256.
2. **Link ⟺ price consistency.** Every filled price must have a link and vice versa,
   per column. A mismatch means a partial write or a stale value.

Then open at least one generated link per site in a real browser and confirm the
rendered price and variant match the cell.
